CHAPTER

  

Strings and Regular Expressions

12

  

This chapter elaborates on the handling of strings in Python. The string object uses ordinary string value. Other than ordinary strings, raw strings can be handled with regular expression. Regular expressions are used to handle text patterns efficiently.

12.1 Methods of String Objects

1. In Python programming, there is no character primitive data type. The character data is also considered as string data with size 1. The string data can be defined with single quote or double quotes.

>>> a=”hello”

>>> b=’hello’

>>> a

‘hello’

>>> b

‘hello’

>>> type(a)

<class ‘str’>

>>> type(b)

<class ‘str’>

2. The string content can be accessed with the index and starts with 0.

>>> a[0]

‘h’

>>> a[0:2]

‘he’

>>> a[3:6]

‘lo’

>>> a[1:4]

‘ell’

3. The elements of a string cannot be deleted. However, the complete string object can be deleted by using ‘del’ keyword.

>>> a=”this is string”

>>> a

‘this is string’

>>> a[0]

‘t’

>>> del(a[0])

Traceback (most recent call last):

File “<stdin>”, line 1, in <module>

TypeError: ‘str’ object doesn’t support item deletion

>>> del a

>>> a

Traceback (most recent call last):

File “<stdin>”, line 1, in <module>

NameError: name ‘a’ is not defined

4. The slice operator “:” is used to extract the characters in the input string. For this, the starting position of the string and the length should be specified.

>>> st=’abcd’

>>> st

‘abcd’

>>> st[1:2]

‘b’

>>> st[0:3]

‘abc’

5. Two strings can be concatenated by using “+” operator. The “*” operator is used to repeat the string.

>>> a=”good”

>>> b=”morning”

>>> a

‘good’

>>> b

‘morning’

>>> c=a+b

>>> c

‘goodmorning’

>>> d=a*2

>>> d

‘goodgood’

6. Two strings can be concatenated by placing the strings together. They can also be combined by using parenthesis. In these cases, the strings should be defined in the current statement only.

>>> ‘good’’morning’

‘goodmorning’

>>> (‘good’

... ‘morning’)

‘goodmorning’

7. In a string object, the index and the character is given as a pair by the enumerate() method.

a=input(“Enter the input string:”)

b=list(enumerate(a))

print(a)

print(b)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the input string: this is a prg

this is a prg

[(0, ‘t’), (1, ‘h’), (2, ‘i’), (3, ‘s’), (4, ‘ ‘),

(5, ‘i’), (6, ‘s’), (7, ‘ ‘), (8, ‘a’), (9, ‘ ‘),

(10, ‘p’), (11, ‘r’), (12, ‘g’)]

8. The len() returns the length of the object.

>>> a=”good morning”

>>> a

‘good morning’

>>> len(a)

12

Various Python string methods are listed below.

■ The string first character is capitalized with capitalize() method.

>>> a

‘good morning’

>>> a.capitalize()

‘Good morning’

■ The center() method will place the input string at the center in the prescribed width.

>>> a

‘good morning’

>>> a.center(15)

‘ good morning ‘

■ The count() method is used to count the frequency occurrence of the input substring in the input string.

>>> a

‘good morning’

>>> a.count(‘o’)

3

■ The endswith() function will check whether the input string ends with the input substring or not.

>>> a

‘good morning’

>>> a.endswith(‘ng’)

True

■ The isalpha() method checks whether all the characters in the string are empty or not. If any one of the character is not an alphabet, then it returns FALSE; otherwise it returns TRUE.

>>> a

‘good morning’

>>> a.isalpha()

False

>>> b=»hai»

>>> b.isalpha()

True

■ The isdecimal()method returns TRUE if all the characters in the string are decimal; otherwise it returns FALSE.

>>> z=”100”

>>> z.isdecimal()

True

■ The isupper() method checks whether or not the all characters in the input string are in upper case. The islower() method checks whether or not all characters in the input string are in lower case or not.

>>> q=”ABC”

>>> q.isupper()

True

>>> q.islower()

False

■ The lower() method will return the string with all the lower case letters, whereas the upper() method will return the string with all the upper case letters.

>>> q=”ABC”

>>> w=q.lower()

>>> w

‘abc’

>>> x=w.upper()

>>> x

‘ABC’

■ The partition() method will partition the input string into various parts. The partition is performed according to the substring.

>>> a=”this is a program”

>>> a

‘this is a program’

>>> a.partition(“ “)

(‘this’, ‘ ‘, ‘is a program’)

>>> a.partition(“s “)

(‘thi’, ‘s ‘, ‘is a program’)

■ The swapcase() method will return the string by swapping lower case to uppercase and upper case to lower case.

>>> z=”ABcdEFgh”

>>> z

‘ABcdEFgh’

>>> z.swapcase()

‘abCDefGH’

12.1.1 Escape sequencing

The escape sequencing character is used to represent special characters in a string. Python supports escape sequencing. Various characters used for escape sequencing are listed below

■ In the string “\” can be represented with “\\”.

>>> a=”ab\”

File “<stdin>”, line 1

a=”ab\”

^

SyntaxError: EOL while scanning string literal

>>> a=”ab\\”

>>> a

‘ab\\’

>>> len(a)

3

■ In string, the single quote can be represented with “\’”.

>>> a=’ab’’

File “<stdin>”, line 1

a=’ab’’

^

SyntaxError: EOL while scanning string literal

>>> a=’ab\’’

>>> a

“ab’”

■ The escape sequence character \” is used to represent the double quote.

>>> x=”ab””

File “<stdin>”, line 1

x=”ab””

^

SyntaxError: EOL while scanning string literal

>>> x=”ab\””

>>> x

‘ab”’

■ “\a” is used to represent the ASCII Bell

>>> z=”a\ab”

>>> z

‘a\x07b’

■ “\b” is used to represent the ASCII backspace.

>>> z=”qw\ber”

>>> z

‘qw\x08er’

■ “\f” is used to represent the ASCII Formfeed.

>>> z=”qw\fer”

>>> z

‘qw\x0cer’

■ “\n” is used to represent the ASCII Linefeed

>>> z=”qw\ner”

>>> z

‘qw\ner’

■ “\r” is used to represent the ASCII carriage return

>>> z=”qw\rer”

>>> z

‘qw\rer’

■ “\t” is used to represent the ASCII horizontal tab.

>>> z=”qw\ter”

>>> z

‘qw\ter’

■ “\v” is used to represent the ASCII vertical tab.

>>> z=”qw\ver”

>>> z

‘qw\x0ber’

12.2 Iterating Strings

In Python, iteration can be applied on the strings using “for” loop.

st=”hello!”

print(“Iterating strings:”)

for i in st:

print(i)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python s1.py

Iterating strings:

h

e

l

l

o

12.3 String Module

The string module has several functions to handle Python strings. The module contains various constants. They are given below.

■ The “ascii_letters” represents both the uppercase and lowercase letters.

■ The “ascii_uppercase” represents the uppercase letters.

■ The “ascii_lowercase” represents the lowercase letters.

■ The “digits” represents the decimal digits.

■ The “hexdigits” represents the hexadecimal digits.

■ The “octdigits” represents the octagonal digits.

■ The “punctuation” represents the possible letters used for punctuation.

■ The “printable” represents the printable letters.

■ The “whitespace” represents the characters used for whitespace.

import string

print(“The value of digits constant: “,string.digits)

print(“The value of letters constant: “,string.ascii_letters)

print(“The value of lowercase constant: “,string.ascii_lowercase)

print(“The value of uppercase constant: “,string.ascii_uppercase)

print(“The value of hexdigits constant: “,string.hexdigits)

print(“The value of octdigits constant: “,string.octdigits)

print(“The value of punctuation constant: “,string.punctuation)

print(“The value of whitespace constant: “,string.whitespace)

print(“The value of printable constant: “,string.printable)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

The value of digits constant: 0123456789

The value of letters constant: abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyzABCDEFGHIJKLMNOPQRSTUVWXYZ

The value of lowercase constant: abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz

The value of uppercase constant: ABCDEFGHIJKLMNOPQRSTUVWXYZ

The value of hexdigits constant: 0123456789abcdefABCDEF

The value of octdigits constant: 01234567

The value of punctuation constant: !”#$%&’()*+,-./:;<=>?@[\]^_`{|}~

The value of whitespace constant:

The value of printable constant: 0123456789abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyzABCDEFGHIJKLMNOPQRSTUVWXYZ!”#$%&’()*+,-./:;<=>?@[\]^_`{|}~

In Python 3, all these functions are available as string methods. Various functions in this module are listed below.

■ The startswith() method is used to check whether the given substring is started within the input string or not. For this, the start and end index can also be specified within the method.

s=input(“Enter the input string:”)

s1=input(“enter the substring to be matched:”)

a=s.startswith(s1)

if(a==1):

print(s1,” is started in “, s)

else:

print(s1,” is not started in “, s)

Output

enter the substring to be matched: good

good is started in good morning

enter the substring to be matched: mor

12.4 String Formatting

Similar to C Programming, Python programming also offers usage of various format specifiers. Various format specifiers are listed below.

■ The %d is used for formatting the integer data.

a=int(input(“Enter integer value:”))

print(“Integer value: %d” %a)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter integer value:10

Integer value: 10

■ The %f is used for formatting the floating point data.

a=float(input(“Enter floating point value:”))

print(“Floating Point value: %f” %a)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter floating point value:25.344

Floating Point value: 25.344000

■ The %.<size>f is used to format the floating with the fixed size of fractional number.

a=float(input(“Enter floating point value:”))

print(“Floating Point value: %f” %a)

print(“Floating Point value: %.2f” %a)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter floating point value:100.56

Floating Point value: 100.560000

Floating Point value: 100.56

■ The %s is used to format the string objects.

a=input(“Enter String value:”)

print(“String value: %s” %a)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter String value:hello

String value: hello

■ The %s is also used to handle other data objects.

b=[100,200,300]

print(“b value: %s” %b)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

b value: [100, 200, 300]

Specifying the position of the string

Python uses format()method to format the input string according to the user requirement. The format()method uses placeholders ({}) for specifying the position of the string. There are four types of arguments as listed below.

■ Default arguments: It takes the arguments according to their order. No need to specify the index of the argument.

■ Positional arguments: It takes the arguments according to their position. The initial argument index starts with 0.

■ Keyword arguments: It takes the arguments based on the keywords.

■ Mixed arguments: It takes the both the positional and keyword arguments.

a=int(input(“Enter the Year: “))

b=input(“Enter the Month Name: “)

c=int(input(“Enter the Date:”))

print(“Default argument data:\n year {}, month {} date {}.”.format(a,b,c))

print(“Positional argument data:\n year {0}, month {1} date {2}.”.format(a,b,c))

print(“Keyword argument data:\n year {x}, month {y} date {z}.”.format(x=a,z=c,y=b))

print(“Mixed argument data:\n year {0}, month {1} date {z}.”.format(a,b,z=c))

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the Year: 2015

Enter the Month Name: sep

Enter the Date:25

Default argument data:

year 2015, month sep date 25.

Positional argument data:

year 2015, month sep date 25.

Keyword argument data:

year 2015, month sep date 25.

Mixed argument data:

year 2015, month sep date 25.

In case of mixed arguments, the positional arguments should be placed before the keyword arguments.

12.5 Regular Expression

Python programming supports a feature called as “regular expressions”. With this, text patterns can be efficiently handled. They are the basic building blocks for machine text processing. Python is using REGREX to support regular expression. For this, input data is given as a string. Within the input string, the required pattern is analyzed. In Python programming, “re” module is used to handle various functions for regular expressions.

12.5.1 Re-module

The “re” module is mainly used to provide various functions for regular expressions. Each of these functions takes two arguments. They are 1) the input string and 2) the pattern.

The input string used by the regular expression is not an ordinary string. An ordinary string contains escape sequence characters. To overcome this, the regular expressions are maintained by “raw strings”. The raw string starts with “r” character followed by the opening single quote.

>>> a=”hel\tlo”

>>> a

‘hel\tlo’

>>> print(a)

hel lo

>>> a=r’hel\tlo’

>>> print(a)

hel\tlo

In Python programming, the patterns need not be characters. Various patterns are listed below.

■ The exact match of ordinary characters is represented by pattern a,X,9,<

■ Any single character other than “\n” is represented by pattern . (a period)

■ Any single word character pattern is \w.

■ Any non word character pattern is \W.

■ Boundary pattern between word and non word is \b.

■ Single white space character pattern is \s.

■ Non single white space character pattern is \S.

■ Pattern for tab is \t

■ Pattern for new line is \n

■ Pattern for return is \r.

■ Decimal digit pattern is \d.

■ Pattern for special character is \special character

■ ^ is used for matching the beginning of the line.

■ $ is used for matching the end of the line.

■ [...] is used for matching any single character in the bracket.

■ [^...] is used for matching any single character not in the bracket.
Various operators can be applicable to the pattern. They are

■ The “+” operator is used to represent one or more repetitions of the pattern to its left.

■ The “*” operator is used represent zero or more repetitions of the pattern to its left.

■ The “?” is used to match the zero or one repetitions of the pattern to its left.
Various functions available in the “re” module are listed below.

■ re.match ()
The syntax is

match_obj=re.match(pattern,string,flags)

o The pattern is a regular expression with raw string

o The string represents the data to be matched with the pattern

o The flags represent the modifiers. For example, re.I is used for case in sensitive matching.

o The match_obj maintains the groups matched.

import re

a = “this is regular expression program and is a match program”

m = re.match( r’(.*)\s(.*)\s(.*)’, a)

if m:

print(“m.group() : “, m.group())

print(“m.group(1) : “, m.group(1))

print(“m.group(2) : “, m.group(2))

print(“m.group(3) : “, m.group(3))

else:

print(“No match found”)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

m.group() : this is regular expression program and is a match program

m.group(1) : this is regular expression program and is a

m.group(2) : match

m.group(3) : program

■ re.search()

The match() will perform checking operation only at the beginning of the string where as the search() will perform checking operation within the string. The syntax of the search() function is

search_obj=re.search(pattern,string,flags)

import re

a = “this is regular expression program and is a match program”

m = re.search( r’(i.*)\s(.*)\s(.*)’, a)

if m:

print(“m.group() : “, m.group())

print(“m.group(1) : “, m.group(1))

print(“m.group(2) : “, m.group(2))

print(“m.group(3) : “, m.group(3))

else:

print(“No match found”)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

m.group() : is is regular expression program and is a match program

m.group(1) : is is regular expression program and is a

m.group(2) : match

m.group(3) : program

The above program with match() function will not find anything and the result is “No match found”.

■ re.sub()

The sub() is used to substitute the new sub string in the input string matched with the pattern. The syntax is

sub_obj=re.sub(pattern,newstring,string,max=0)

import re

a = “this is regular expression program and is a match program”

m = re.sub( r’(i.*)\s(.*)\s(.*)’, “”,a)

print(“After matching and substitution result: “,m)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

After matching and substitution result: th

■ re.split()

The split() function will partition the input text into various parts based on delimiters.

import re

a = “this is regular expression program and is a match program”

m = re.split( r’(.*)\s(e.*)’, a)

print(“After matching and substitution result: “)

for i in m:

print(i)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

After matching and substitution result:

this is regular

expression program and is a match program

■ re.findall()

It is similar to split() function except that partition is not performed based on delimiter. Other than this, the partitioning is performed based on the matching.

import re

a = “this is regular expression program and is a match program”

m = re.findall( r’(.*)\s(e.*)’, a)

print(“After matching and substitution result: “)

for i in m:

print(i)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

After matching and substitution result:

(‘this is regular’, ‘expression program and is a match program’)

12.6 Dictionary-based Formatting Expressions

Dictionary elements can also be placed as part of a string. For this, “%” operator is used to represent the elements of the dictionary.

di={“a”:1,”b”:2.6,”c”:”hai”}

st1=”%(a)d”%di

st2=”%(b)f”%di

st3=”%(c)s”%di

print(st1)

print(st2)

print(st3)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python bb.py

1

2.600000

hai

Program 6: Write a Python program to find the frequency of characters occurring in a string?

s=input(“Enter the input string: “)

res=[]

du=set()

u=[]

for l in s:

if l not in du:

u.append(l)

du.add(l)

for i in range(0,len(u)):

t=u[i]

v=0

for j in range(0,len(s)):

if(t==s[j]):

v=v+1;

res.append(v)

print(“Input string= “,s)

print(“Frequency Occurrence= “,res)

for i in x:

if(x.count(i)==1):

print(“The first non repeated chracter in the string: “,i)

break

Enter the string:aaabccc

The first non repeated chracter in the string: b

Program 18: Write a Python program to print the run length encoding.

x=input(“Enter the string:”)

s=’’

for i in x:

if(i not in s):

s=s+i

s=s+str(x.count(i))

print(“original string: “,x)

print(“The run length code: “,s)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python b2.py

Enter the string:aaaabbbcccccc

original string: aaaabbbcccccc

The run length code: a4b3c6

print(“The two strings are not anagrams”)

Glossary

■ The string object uses ordinary string value.

■ Raw strings can be handled with regular expression.

■ Regular expressions are used to handle text patterns efficiently.

Summary

■ The string is used to represent character data.

■ Each character of the string is accessed with the corresponding index value.

■ The string can be iterated. Various operations can be performed on the string object.

■ The string module provides various functions for handling strings in Python.

■ Other than ordinary strings, raw strings can be efficiently handled by the regular expression.

■ The Re module is used for performing various operations with the regular expressions.

CHAPTER

  

Files and Directory Access

13

  

Python programming can handle various operations on a file by using streams. There are two different streams, viz., I/O and file streams. The present chapter discusses about various operations performed on a file.

13.1 Files and Streams

A file contains data stored in memory. All programming languages provide file handling features. Python also supports various file handling operations. Python uses streams for handling files. Stream represents continuous flow of data. There are two types of streams. They are

■ Input/Output (I/O) stream

■ File stream

The I/O stream is used to handle various operations related to input devices and output devices. The input stream is used to handle the input devices. input is used to represent the input stream. print is used to represent the output stream.

a=input(“Enter the input for input: “)

print(“Entered String: “,a)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the input for raw_input: 1+2

Entered String: 1+2

A file can be created, deleted or modified using only the file stream. Import of module is not necessary to use the file stream. The file object has various attributes as given below.

■ The “closed” attribute represents whether the input file object is closed or not.

■ The “mode” attribute represents the mode of the file

■ The “name” attribute represents the name of the file.

13.2 Opening a File

In Python, open() function is used to open a file. This function will take two arguments. The first argument represents the name of the file. The second argument represents the mode of the file to be opened. The mode of the file can be any one of the following

■ Read

■ Write

■ Append

13.2.1 The read mode

The read mode allows performing read operations on the input file. The various types of read mode are

■ r mode is used to open the file for reading purpose only. This is the default mode for opening any file. In this case, the file pointer is always placed at the starting point of the file.

■ r+ mode is used to open the file for performing both read and write operations. In this case, the file pointer is always placed at the starting point of the file.

■ rb mode is used to open the file for reading it in binary format. In this case, the file pointer is always placed at the starting point of the file.

■ rb+ mode is used to open the file for performing both read and write operations in only binary format. In this case, the file pointer is always placed at the starting point of the file.

Content of abc.text

This is first line in abc.

f=open(“abc.txt”,”r”)

for line in f:

print(line)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

This is first line in abc.

13.2.2 The write mode

The write mode is used to perform the write operation in the file. Various types of write mode are

■ w+ mode is used to create a new file to perform both reading and writing operations. If the file already exists, then the file is overwritten. In this case, the file pointer is always placed at the starting point of the file.

■ w mode is used to create a new file to perform only writing operation. If the file already exists then the file is overwritten. In this case, the file pointer is always placed at the starting point of the file.

■ wb mode is used create a new file to perform only writing operation in binary format. If the file already exists, then the file is overwritten. In this case, the file pointer is always placed at the starting point of the file.

■ wb+ mode is used to create a new file to perform both reading and writing operations in binary format. If the file already exists then the file is overwritten. In this case, the file pointer is always placed at the starting point of the file.

Content of abc.text

This is first line in abc.

f=open(“abc.txt”,”w+”)

f.write(“This is second line abc.txt”)

Output

Content of abc.text

This is second line abc.txt

In this example, the file abc.txt is already available. So, the w+ mode overwrites the existing file with the given content. If the file is not available, then it creates a new file.

13.2.3 The append mode

The append mode is used to append the data at the end of the file. If the file is already available, then the data is appended at the end of the file; otherwise a new file will be created and the content will be appended to the new file.

■ a mode is used to open the file for appending purpose only. In this case, the file pointer is always placed at the end point of the file.

■ a+ mode is used to open the file for performing both read and append operations. In this case, the file pointer is always placed at the end point of the file.

■ ab mode is used to open the file for appending it in binary format. In this case, the file pointer is always placed at the end point of the file.

■ ab+ mode is used to open the file for performing both read and append operations in only binary format. In this case, the file pointer is always placed at the end point of the file.

Content of abc.text

This is second line abc.txt

f=open(“abc.txt”,”a”)

f.write(“\nThis is third line abc.txt”)

Output

Content of abc.text

This is second line abc.txt

This is third line abc.txt

13.3 Reading/Writing Operations on a File

The open()function is used to open the file. The mode in which the file is opened decides which operation is to be performed in the file. In a file, we can perform two operations. They are

■ Read

■ Write

13.3.1 The read operation

1. The read operation is performed with the read() method. The read() method should be used with the file stream object.

f=open(“abc.txt”)

s=f.read()

print(s)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

This is second line abc.txt

This is third line abc.txt

The read() method reads the data from the beginning of the file. It reads the data and returns it as string object. For read() method, we can also specify the size limit of the data to be read.

f=open(“abc.txt”)

s=f.read(6)

print(s)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

This i

In the above example code, only 6 string objects (characters) are returned from “abc.txt” by the read() method.

2. The readline() method is used to return the line-by-line data from the input file.

f=open(“abc.txt”)

s=f.readline()

print(s)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

This is second line abc.txt

2. The readlines() method is used to return all lines of data from the input file.

f=open(“abc.txt”)

s=f.readlines()

print(s)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python bb.py

[‘This is second line abc.txt\n’, ‘This is third line abc.txt\n’]

13.3.2 The write operation

1. Similar to read() method, the write() method is used to write the data into the file. If the file is opened in the write mode, then the write() method starts the writing operation from the beginning of the file; otherwise the write() method starts writing from the end of the file.

Content of abc.text

This is second line abc.txt

This is third line abc.txt

Output

Content of abc.text

This is a Python programming book

f=open(“abc.txt”,”w”)

s=”This is a Python programming book”

f.write(s)

2. The writelines() method is used to write multiple lines of data at a time into the file. If the file is opened in the write mode, then the writelines() method starts the writing operation from the beginning of the file; otherwise the writelines() method starts writing from the end of the file.

Content of abc.text

This is second line abc.txt

This is third line abc.txt

Output

Content of abc.text

This is a Python programming book

This is file chapter

f=open(“abc.txt”,”w”)

s=[“This is a Python programming book”, “\nThis is file chapter”]

f.writelines(s)

13.4 Other File Operations

Other than read and write operations, various other operations are also allowed. They are given below.

■ The close() method is used to close the file object that is already created.

f1=open(“abc.txt”)

s2=f1.read()

print(“The data from file:”)

print(s2)

f1.close()

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

The data from file:

This is a Python programming book

This is file chapter

■ The rename() method is used to rename the existing file name with a new file name. This method is available in os module. So, to use this function, the os module should be imported.

import os

f1=open(“abc.txt”)

print(“Name of the file: “,f1.name)

f1.close()

os.rename(“abc.txt”,”h1.txt”)

print(“Opening with new name”)

f1=open(“h1.txt”)

print(“Name of the file: “,f1.name)

f1.close()

print(“Opening with old name:”)

f1=open(“abc.txt”)

print(“Name of the file: “,f1.name)

f1.close()

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Name of the file: abc.txt

Opening with new name

Name of the file: h1.txt

Opening with old name:

Traceback (most recent call last):

File “a1.py”, line 15, in <module>

f1=open(“abc.txt”)

FileNotFoundError: [Errno 2] No such file or directory: ‘abc.txt’

■ The os module also contains remove() function. This function deletes the input file. To use this function, os module should be imported.

import os

print(“Opening with new name”)

f1=open(“h1.txt”)

print(“Name of the file: “,f1.name)

f1.close()

os.remove(“h1.txt”)

print(“Opening the deleted file:”)

f1=open(“h1.txt”)

print(“Name of the file: “,f1.name)

f1.close()

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Opening with new name

Name of the file: h1.txt

Opening the deleted file:

Traceback (most recent call last):

File “a1.py”, line 12, in <module>

f1=open(“h1.txt”)

FileNotFoundError: [Errno 2] No such file or

directory: ‘h1.txt’

■ The tell() method is used to represent the current position of the file object.

Content of hello.text

This is first line

This is second line

This is third line

f3=open(“hello.txt”,”a”)

x=f3.tell()

f3.write(“ abc”)

print(“the current position of f3: “,x)

f3.close()

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

the current position of f3: 61

Content of hello.text

This is first line

This is second line

This is third line abc

■ The seek() method is used to relocate current position of a file object to the specified position. It contains two arguments, as listed below.

o The first argument specifies the number of bytes to be moved.

o The second argument specifies the position from which seek() method should work.

Generally, it can move from beginning, current or ending position of the file. The corresponding values are {0,1,2}. These are defined as constants in os module. The names of the constants are SEEK_SET, SEEK_CUR and SEEK_END.

Content of hello.text

This is first line

This is second line

This is third line

import os

f3=open(“hello.txt”,”a”)

x=f3.tell()

print(“the current position of f3: “,x)

f3.write(“ abc”)

x=f3.tell()

print(“the current position of f3: “,x)

f3.seek(5,os.SEEK_SET)

x=f3.tell()

print(“the current position of f3: “,x)

f3.close()

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

the current position of f3: 61

the current position of f3: 65

the current position of f3: 5

Content of hello.text

This is first line

This is second line

This is third line abc

■ The truncate() function is used to delete the content of a file. The deletion is performed from the specified position.

Content of hello.text

This is first line

This is second line

This is third line

f3=open(“hello.txt”,”r+”)

f3.truncate(10)

y=f3.read()

f3.close()

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

The data from file:

This is fi

Content of hello.text

This is fi

13.5 Iterating through Files

In Python programming, the os module provides several methods for iterating through all files in the directory. These methods are listed below.

■ The os.listdir() method returns all components within the specified directory. It can also display all the files within the specified directory. The example code below displays the names of the files available in the subdirectory aa.

import os

for f in os.listdir(‘./aa’):

if os.path.isfile(f):

print (“Name of the file: “,f)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Name of the file: a2.py

Name of the file: bitwise.py

Name of the file: del.py

■ The os.walk() method is used to walk all the components within the specified directory. The example code below displays the names of all components within the current directory.

import os

for root, dirs, filenames in os.walk(‘.’):

for f in filenames:

print (“Name of the component: “,f)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Name of the component: get-pip.py

Name of the component: hello.txt

Name of the component: new.txt

Name of the component: __pycache__

13.6 Splitting Words

In Python, the words in the file can be split by using regular expressions. After iterating the result of the regular expression, the frequency occurrence of each word can be estimated.

import re

import string

f = {}

d = open(‘one.txt’, ‘r’)

t = d.read().lower()

m = re.findall(r’\b[a-z]{3,15}\b’, t)

for w in m:

c = f.get(w,0)

f[w] = c + 1

f_list = f.keys()

for w in f_list:

print(w, f[w])

one.txt

hello this is first line

hai this is second line

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python s1.py

hello 1

this 2

first 1

line 2

hai 1

second 1

13.7 Serialization and De-serialization

Serialization is a process used to reduce data loss. In this process, the data is not maintained with their names, but with the objects. The de-serialization process is the reverse process of serialization. It ungroups data from the corresponding object. In Python programming, the serialization and de-serialization process is performed with the help of pickle module. This module contains various methods for serialization and de-serialization of Python objects. In the serialization process, the objects are converted into a byte stream and in the de-serialization process, the byte stream is converted into objects. Various functions in pickle module are given below.

■ The dump() function is used to perform the serialization process.

import pickle

fruits = [“apple”, “orange”, “banana”, “grape”,”papya”]

fh = open(“ser1.txt”, “wb”)

pickle.dump(fruits, fh)

print(“Serilization process is completed”)

fh.close()

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Serilization process is completed

■ The de-serialization process is performed with the load() function.

import pickle

file1 = open(“ser1.txt”, “rb”)

f = pickle.load(file1)

print(“The de-serialized data: “)

print(f)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a2.py

The de-serialized data:

[‘apple’, ‘orange’, ‘banana’, ‘grape’, ‘papya’]

13.8 Hash Files

Hash functions are used to provide security to the files. The hash function will take the variable length file as an input and generate a fixed-length hash code. The generated hash code is called as Message Digest. The Python programming provides support for two types of hash functions viz., MD5 and SHA-1. For this, Python provides hashlib module for handling hashing functions. To generate the digest for any type of file, it is mandatory that the file should be read in binary mode. The below code discusses about md5 and sha-1 functions.

import hashlib

h = hashlib.md5()

with open(‘one.txt’, ‘rb’) as f1:

b= f1.read()

h.update(b)

print(“message Digest for one.txt file:”)

print(h.hexdigest())

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python s1.py

message Digest for one.txt file:

8fc2cf39909a8164eac88ab924781de8

import hashlib

h = hashlib.sha1()

with open(‘one.txt’, ‘rb’) as f1:

b= f1.read()

h.update(b)

print(“message Digest for one.txt file:”)

print(h.hexdigest())

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python s1.py

message Digest for one.txt file:

2117821b56253165e37b145547e37309ebf304fe

13.9 Directory Access

The directory contains multiple files. In Python programming, the os module provides various functions to handle the operations of the directory. These functions are listed below.

■ The mkdir() function is used to create a new directory in the current directory.

import os

os.mkdir(“newdir”)

print(“New directory is created”)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

New directory is created

■ The chdir() function is used to change the directory from current to the specified directory.

import os

os.chdir(“newdir”)

print(“changed to newdir directory”)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

changed to newdir directory

■ The getcwd() function is used to get the name of the current working directory.

import os

os.chdir(“newdir”)

print(“changed to newdir directory”)

print(“current directory: “,os.getcwd())

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

changed to newdir directory

current directory: D:\PythonPrograms\newdir

■ The rmdir() function is used to remove the specified directory.

import os

os.rmdir(“newdir”)

print(“newdir directory is deleted”)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

newdir directory is deleted

■ The content of the directory can be accessed with the help of glob() defined in glob package.

import glob

ac = glob.glob(“d:\\Python\\**\\**”)

print(“Content of Python directory:”)

print(ac)

Output

Program 3: Write a Python program to write and read the data in the input file.

n=int(input(“Enter number of lines in the data: “))

s=[]

print(“Enter the data line by line”)

for i in range(n):

s1=input(“Enter data: “)

s.append(s1)

s.append(“\n”)

Program 4: Write a Python program to read and write with a single file object.

import os

f3=open(“new.txt”,”w+”)

x=f3.tell()

print(“the current position: “,x)

f3.write(“ this is first line in new.txt”)

x=f3.tell()

print(“the current position: “,x)

f3.seek(0,os.SEEK_SET)

x=f3.tell()

print(“the current position: “,x)

y=f3.read()

print(“The data from file:”)

print(y)

x=f3.tell()

print(“the current position: “,x)

f3.close()

When the file is opened in the write mode, the file pointer is at the starting position of the file. After performing the write operation, the file moves to the end of the file. So, if a file is opened in the w+ mode, then we can perform both read and write operations. The file object is positioned initially at the beginning of the file. After performing the write operation, the current position indicates the end of the file. Then to perform the read operation, no data is found from the end of the file position. Hence, the current position should be moved to the specified location. For this, seek() method is used.

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

the current position: 0

the current position: 30

the current position: 0

The data from file: this is first line in new.txt

the current position: 30

Program 5: Write a Python program to serialize and de-serialize multiple objects.

Serialization process: (a1.py)

import pickle

fruits1 = [“apple”, “orange”, “banana”, “grape”,”papya”]

colors=[“red”,”green”,”blue”]

fh = open(“ser1.txt”, “wb”)

x=(fruits1,colors)

pickle.dump(x, fh)

print(“Serilization process is completed”)

fh.close()

De-serialization process: (a2.py)

import pickle

file1 = open(“ser1.txt”, “rb”)

(x,y) = pickle.load(file1)

print(“The de-serialized data: “)

print(x)

print(y)

[‘red’, ‘green’, ‘blue’]

Program 6: Write a Python program to display each word of a file in reverse order.

with open(‘a.txt’, ‘r’) as myfile:

a1=data.split()

print(“reverse words:”)

for i in a1:

r=’’

for j in range(len(i)-1,-1,-1):

r=r+i[j]

print(r,end=” “)

Glossary

■ A stream represents the flow of data. The I/O stream represents the flow of data towards I/O devices and file stream represents the flow of data towards files.

■ The os module provides various methods for iterating through all files in the directory.

■ Serialization is a process used to reduce data loss.

Summary

■ The input stream is used to handle the various operations of input devices.

■ The output stream is used to handle the various operations of output devices.

■ The file stream is used to handle various operations on files.

■ The file object is used to handle the given file.

■ The os module provides various operations for the files.

■ Serialization and de-serialization processes can be performed in Python programming.

CHAPTER

  

Errors and Exceptions

14

  

In Python, errors and exceptions are handled efficiently. The try, except and else blocks are used to handle the exceptions. The finally block is used to execute the set of statements without checking the condition of the exception. The present chapter discusses in detail about errors and exceptions.

14.1 Motivation

During the execution of a program, there may be a possibility of perceiving errors. There are three types of errors. They are

■ Syntax error

■ Logic error

■ Exception

Syntax errors are errors generated during the parsing. So, they are also called as parsing errors. In the program, if there is any syntax error, then the program will not be executed.

x=input(“Enter data: “)

print(“Entered data:”

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

File “a1.py”, line 4

^

SyntaxError: unexpected EOF while parsing

The above example code has a syntax error. The print statement does not end with “)”. So, during the program parsing, this error is generated and the program is not executed.

The second type of error is called as logic error. Logic errors do not interrupt the execution of the program but they are responsible for getting unwanted result. Fixing logic errors has been found to be difficult.

a=int(input(“Enter the first number:”))

b=int(input(“Enter the second number:”))

c=a-b;

print(“The first number:”,a)

print(“The second number:”,b)

print(“Result of addition:”,c)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python s1.py

Enter the first number:10

Enter the second number:3

The first number: 10

The second number: 3

Result of addition: 7

The above program is intended to perform the addition of given two numbers. But due to logical error, the subtraction result is obtained.

The third type of error is called as “exception”. Exceptions are the errors generated during the run time. During the execution of the program, if the exception is generated, then the execution is terminated immediately.

x=int(input(“Enter number1: “))

y=int(input(“Enter number2: “))

print(“Number1 value: “,x)

print(“Number2 value: “,y)

print(“result of number1/number2: “,x/y)

print(“result of number1*number2: “,x*y)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter number1: 15

Enter number2: 0

Number1 value: 15

Number2 value: 0

Traceback (most recent call last):

File “a1.py”, line 7, in <module>

print(“result of number1/number2: “,x/y)

ZeroDivisionError: division by zero

In the above example code, there is no syntax error. So, the program execution is started. During the execution process, the operation “15/0” has to be executed. This is an error such that the result is infinity. This value cannot be represented. To overcome this problem, a runtime error called as Exception “ZeroDivisionError” is generated.

14.2 Examples of Exception

Some examples of exceptions in Python are listed below.

■ When the floating point calculation fails, then FloatingPointError will be generated.

■ When an error is generated during the arithmetic operations then ArithmeticError will be generated.

■ When an import statement is failed, then ImportError will be generated.

■ In the dictionary, if the input key is not identified then KeyError will be generated.

■ In the sequence, if the input index is not found then IndexError will be generated.

■ OSError will be generated for any issues in operating system.

14.3 Handling Exceptions

In Python programming, exceptions can be handled efficiently. The exception can be handled by using try block. The try block may contain multiple except statements. The syntax of the try block is

........

try:

......

except exception-n:

......

else:

......

............

In the program, the code that is expected to raise an exception will be placed in the try block. If an exception is raised, then the corresponding except block will be executed. If the exception is not raised in the try block, then the else block will be executed.

14.4 Try Keyword

The syntax of the exception handling mechanism in Python contains three blocks. They are try, except and else blocks.

x=int(input(“Enter the number1:”))

y=int(input(“Enter the number2:”))

print(“The value of number1:”,x)

print(“The value of number2:”,y)

try:

z=x/y

except ZeroDivisionError:

print(“Number2 value is zero”)

else:

print(“The result=”,z)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the number1:15

Enter the number2:0

The value of number1: 15

The value of number2: 0

Number2 value is zero

While executing the above program, the two values entered are 15 and 0. So, the ZeroDivisionError exception can be raised. To handle this exception, except block is executed. If the exception is not raised, then the else block will be executed.

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the number1:15

Enter the number2:3

The value of number1: 15

The value of number2: 3

The result= 5.0

In any Python program, for a single try block, we can write multiple except statements. Each except block handles the corresponding exception.

try:

x=int(input(“Enter the number1:”))

y=int(input(“Enter the number2:”))

print(“The value of number1:”,x)

print(“The value of number2:”,y)

z=x/y

except ZeroDivisionError:

print(“Number2 value is zero”)

except KeyboardInterrupt:

print(“\nuser is interrupted by pressing ctrl+c”)

else:

print(“The result=”,z)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the number1:15

Enter the number2:

user is interrupted by pressing ctrl+c

Now, let us assume that while entering the value of the second data, the user interrupted by pressing ctrl+c. This will raise an exception called as KeyboardInterrupt. This exception is handled by using second except block. This program can also handle the ZeroDivisionError exception.

In Python, multiple exceptions can be declared within a single except block. In this case, multiple exceptions should be specified within() sepearted by “,”.

try:

x=int(input(“Enter the number1:”))

y=int(input(“Enter the number2:”))

print(“The value of number1:”,x)

print(“The value of number2:”,y)

z=x/y

except (ZeroDivisionError,KeyboardInterrupt):

print(“\nException is raised”)

else:

print(“The result=”,z)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the number1:15

Enter the number2:0

The value of number1: 15

The value of number2: 0

Exception is raised

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the number1:87

Enter the number2:

Exception is raised

In Python, except block can be defined such that it can handle any type of exception.

try:

x=int(input(“Enter the number1:”))

y=int(input(“Enter the number2:”))

print(“The value of number1:”,x)

print(“The value of number2:”,y)

z=x/y

except:

print(“\nException is raised”)

else:

print(“The result=”,z)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the number1:54

Enter the number2:0

The value of number1: 54

The value of number2: 0

Exception is raised

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the number1:

Exception is raised

14.5 Finally Keyword

The finally block can also be included within the try block. If an exception is raised within the try block, then except block will be executed; otherwise, the else block will be executed. So, either except or else blocks will be executed for the corresponding try block. The finally block will be executed whether any condition of exception is raised or not. In both of these cases, the finally block code will be executed.

try:

x=int(input(“Enter the number1:”))

y=int(input(“Enter the number2:”))

print(“The value of number1:”,x)

print(“The value of number2:”,y)

z=x/y

except (ZeroDivisionError,KeyboardInterrupt):

print(“\nException is raised”)

else:

print(“The result=”,z)

finally:

print(“This is finally block”)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the number1:25

Enter the number2:5

The value of number1: 25

The value of number2: 5

The result= 5.0

This is finally block

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the number1:25

Enter the number2:

Exception is raised

This is finally block

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the number1:25

Enter the number2:0

The value of number1: 25

The value of number2: 0

Exception is raised

This is finally block

From the above example code output, it may be seen that the finally block is executed irrespective of the condition of the exception.

14.6 Handling Exception in Invoked Functions

Python programming allows the handling of the exceptions raised in the invoked functions. Let us say function A() invokes function B() and function B() invokes function C(). If the exception is raised in the function C(), then the exception handling mechanism handles the raised exception in the tray block of function C() and continues the execution of catch handlers. Then the flow of control is continued for executing functions B() and A().

def x():

print(“Invoking y function”)

y()

print(“y is invoked successfully”)

def y():

print(“Invoking z function”)

z()

print(“z is invoked successfully”)

def z():

try:

x=int(input(“Enter the number1:”))

y=int(input(“Enter the number2:”))

print(“The value of number1:”,x)

print(“The value of number2:”,y)

z=x/y

except (ZeroDivisionError,KeyboardInterrupt):

print(“\nException is raised”)

else:

print(“The result=”,z)

finally:

print(“This is finally block”)

print(“Invoking x function”)

x()

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python b2.py

Invoking x function

Invoking y function

Invoking z function

Enter the number1:10

Enter the number2:0

The value of number1: 10

The value of number2: 0

Exception is raised

This is finally block

z is invoked successfully

y is invoked successfully

14.7 With and Except Statements

The with statement in Python will reduce the complexity of the exception handling mechanism. It wraps all the related tasks of clean ups and required operations. The best usage of with statement is the opening of a file. In this case, there is no need to specify the statements for close operation. The with statement automatically closes the opened file object.

The exceptions raised within the with block can also be handled. For this case, the with block should be placed inside a try block and the corresponding except blocks should be defined.

a=0

b=2

try:

with (b/a) as f:

print(“no exception in with block”)

except:

print(“exception in with block”)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

exception in with block

14.8 Raising Exceptions

The previous sections discussed about handling exceptions that are raised by default. However, in Python programming, the exceptions can also be raised manually. This type of exception is also handled efficiently in Python. The syntax for raising an exception manually is given below.

raise type_of_exception[,value_exception[,object_traceback]]

Manual exceptions are also handled by using except block. Most of these raised exceptions can be objects of a string or class.

try:

a=int(input(“Enter the value of a: “))

b=int(input(“Enter the value of b: “))

print(“The value of a= “,a)

print(“The value of b= “,b)

if(a-b)<0:

raise Exception(“value of a-b is <0”)

except Exception as e:

print(“Received exception: “,e)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the value of a: 15

Enter the value of b: 20

The value of a= 15

The value of b= 20

Received exception: value of a-b is <0

14.9 Re-raising Exception

In Python programming, the last exception can be re-raised. The re-raising of an exception is performed with the raise statement. For this, multiple try-except blocks are used.

try:

a=int(input(“Enter the value of a: “))

b=int(input(“Enter the value of b: “))

print(“The value of a= “,a)

print(“The value of b= “,b)

if(a-b)<0:

raise Exception(“Exception raised”)

except Exception as e:

try:

print(“Received exception: “,e)

if(a<10):

raise e

except Exception as e1:

print(“Received raised exception:”,e)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python s1.py

Enter the value of a: 4

Enter the value of b: 7

The value of a= 4

The value of b= 7

Received exception: Exception raised

Received raised exception: Exception raised

14.10 Instantiating Exception

In Python programming, whenever an exception is raised, an instance of the exception base class will be created. The arguments that are passed during the exception raise will be stored in instance.args and the string is represented with __str__().

try:

a=int(input(“Enter the value of a: “))

b=int(input(“Enter the value of b: “))

print(“The value of a= “,a)

print(“The value of b= “,b)

if(a-b)<0:

raise Exception(“value of a-b is <0”)

except Exception as e:

print(“Exception caught”)

print(“type of instance:”,type(e))

print(“Arguments stored:”,e.args)

print(“__str__() value=”,e)

x=e.args

print(“argument:”,x)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python s1.py

Enter the value of a: 4

Enter the value of b: 8

The value of a= 4

The value of b= 8

Exception caught

type of instance: <class ‘Exception’>

Arguments stored: (‘value of a-b is <0’,)

__str__ value= value of a-b is <0

argument: (‘value of a-b is <0’,)

14.11 Custom Exceptions

In Python programming, there are two types of exceptions. They are

■ Built-in exceptions

■ Custom exceptions

For built-in-exceptions, the definition is available with Python. So, the user need not provide the definition of built-in exceptions. The built-in exceptions can be raised either manually or automatically. Some of the built-in exceptions are listed below.

■ BufferedError – Raised during the execution of buffer related operations.

■ LookupError– Raised when the key or index of a sequence or mapping is invalid.

■ AssertionError– Raised during the failure of assert statement execution

■ AttributeError– Raised for referencing invalid attribute reference.

■ FloatingPointError– Raised during the failure of floating point operation.

■ IOError– Raised during the failure of I/O operation.

■ ImportError– Raised during the failure of import statement.

In Python programming, the definitions of user-defined exceptions should initially be defined within the program for effective handling. Each of the user-defined exception requires its class definition. In general, the class of user-defined exception should be very less in size.

class ne(Exception):

pass

try:

a=int(input(“Enter the value of a: “))

b=int(input(“Enter the value of b: “))

print(“The value of a= “,a)

print(“The value of b= “,b)

if(a-b)<0:

raise ne

except ne:

print(“custom exception is raised”)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the value of a: 5

Enter the value of b: 14

The value of a= 5

The value of b= 14

custom exception is raised

14.12 Assert Statement

To trap the user-defined constants, assert statement is used in Python programming. By using the assert statement, line-by-line debugging will be performed. The assert statement is also called as conditional raise statement. If the condition is not satisfied, then the assert statement will raise an exception. The syntax for assert statement is

assert expression[,argument]

The assert statement is handled by the try...except block. If the asserted exception is not handled, then the program will be immediately terminated.

a=int(input(“Enter the value of a: “))

b=int(input(“Enter the value of b: “))

print(“The value of a= “,a)

print(“The value of b= “,b)

assert a<b, “custom exception is raised”

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the value of a: 9

Enter the value of b: 5

The value of a= 9

The value of b= 5

Traceback (most recent call last):

File “a1.py”, line 8, in <module>

assert a<b, “custom exception is raised”

AssertionError: custom exception is raised

14.13 Pre-defined Clean-up Actions

Pre-defined clean-up actions are used to clean the memory by removing the objects that are not used for a long time.

one.txt

hello

s1.py

for i in open(“one.txt”,”r”):

print(i)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python s1.py

hello

In the above program, the one.txt is open even after the execution of the program. So, to avoid this, the pre-defined clean-up action is performed with the with statement. After this statement is executed, the file will automatically be closed.

with open(“one.txt”,”r”) as x:

for i in x:

print(i)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python s1.py

hello

Program 1: Write a Python program to handle the ImportError exception.

Programming Tip:

Exceptions are raised during the run time.

try:

import os1

except ImportError as e:

print(“Raised Error: “,e)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Raised Error: No module named ‘os1’

Program 2: Write a Python program to handle the IOError and IndexError.

try:

import sys

print(sys.argv[1])

except IOError as e:

print(“Raised Error: “,e)

except IndexError as f:

print(“Raised Error: “,f)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Raised Error: list index out of range

Program 3: Write a Python program to manually raise an exception when the number is positive.

try:

x=int(input(“Enter the number: “))

if(x>0):

raise Exception

except Exception:

print(“Raised Error:”)

else:

print(“The input nuber: “,x)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the number: 5

Raised Error:

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Enter the number: -5

The input nuber: -5

Glossary

■ Syntax errors are errors generated at the time of parsing.

■ Logic errors do not interrupt the execution of the program but they are responsible for getting unwanted results.

■ Exceptions are errors generated during the run time. During the execution of the program, if the exception is generated, then the execution is terminated immediately.

■ The try, except and else blocks are used to handle the exceptions.

■ The finally block is used to execute a set of statements without checking the condition of the exception.

Summary

■ There are three types of errors viz., syntax error, logical error and exception. The syntax errors are also called as parsing errors.

■ Fixing logical errors is difficult. If an exception is raised, then the program execution is automatically terminated.

■ The try block is used to handle the statements that raise exceptions.

■ The raised exceptions can be handled with except block.

■ If the exception is not raised, then the else block is executed.

■ Irrespective of the condition of the exception, the finally block will be executed.

CHAPTER

  

Multithreading

15

  

Each program consists of multiple statements. Execution of each statement can further create multiple processes. Execution of each process, in turn, leads to the creation of multiple threads. Python programming can efficiently handle the execution of multiple threads. A thread is a light-weight process. Multiple threads can share the memory space of the associated process. The present chapter discusses in detail about how multithreading is handled in Python.

15.1 Introduction to Thread

A thread is a single flow of the process. It is used for exploiting the latency and concurrency in parallel, distributed processing. They are mainly intended for designing event-driven software. The thread can be of three types:

■ User thread

■ Kernel thread

■ Hybrid thread

A multithreaded program contains multiple threads that are executed parallely. All these multiple threads will share a common memory space. So, the data is shared among all the multiple threads.

15.2 Differences Between Process and Thread

For executing a program, there are two types of units, viz., process and thread. When a program is executing, multiple processes or threads can be created from itself. A program contains various sets of instructions. For the execution of each instruction, a process is required. In some cases, each process contains multiple threads. The differences between process and thread are listed below.

■ The process can be considered as a heavy-weight task while the thread is a light-weight task. Here, weight represents the memory usage.

■ In case of multiprocessing, each process maintains its own copy of the data in a separate memory space. In case of multithreading, all multiple threads maintain only one copy in memory space, which is shared by all the processes.

■ Multiple processes can easily communicate with one another. However, multiple threads use a complex procedure to communicate with one another.

■ Each process requires maintaining its own copy of process table to maintain the duplicated data from the parent process. Each of the thread shares common memory; so they do not require additional tables.

■ Changes made by one process will not be reflected to other processes, whereas changes made by one thread will be reflected on all other threads.

■ The parent process will have control over the child processes, whereas threads of the same process will have control over one another.

■ Processes are independent, whereas threads are dependent.

15.3 Threading Module

The threading module is used to provide various functions for handling threads in Python. This module considers each thread as an object. Various functions and constants defined in threading module are listed below.

■ The active_count() function returns the number of live threads for the currently executing program.

■ The current_thread() function returns the name of the current thread being executed.

■ The get_ident() function returns the thread identifier.

■ The list of all live multiple threads list is given by the enumerate()function.

■ The name of the main thread is given by the main_thread()function.

■ The maximum time allocated for each of the thread is given by the IMEOUT_MAX constant.

import threading

print(“No. of Alive threads: “,threading.active_count())

print(“Current Thread Object: “,threading.current_thread())

print(“Thread Identifier: “, threading.get_ident())

print(“List of Alive Threads: “,threading.enumerate())

print(“Main Thread Object: “,threading.main_thread())

print(“Thread Max. Time: “, threading.TIMEOUT_MAX)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

No. of Alive threads: 1

Current Thread Object: <_MainThread(MainThread, started 5964)>

Thread Identifier: 5964

List of Alive Threads: [<_MainThread(MainThread, started 5964)>]

Main Thread Object: <_MainThread(MainThread, started 5964)>

Thread Max. Time: 4294967.0

15.4 Thread Synchronization

■ Local object information can be obtained with the usage of local() function.

import threading

x=threading.local()

print(“Local Thread Object: “,x)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Local Thread Object: <_thread._local object at 0x03887AB0>

■ In earlier versions of Python, two modules, thread and threading, are available. But in Python3, _thread and threading modules are available. All the functions of a thread are available in threading module.

>>> import thread

Traceback (most recent call last):

File “<stdin>”, line 1, in <module>

ModuleNotFoundError: No module named ‘thread’

>>> import _thread

>>>

■ In Python programming, a new thread can be created in two different ways. They are

o By using _thread module

o By using threading module

In case of _thread module, a new thread can be created by defining the action of the thread in a function. The syntax is given below

_thread.start_new_thread(function_name_obj,(arg1,arg2...))

import _thread

def abc(arg1):

print(“Thread Name: “,arg1)

_thread.start_new_thread(abc,(“Thread1”,))

_thread.start_new_thread(abc,(“Thread2”,))

_thread.start_new_thread(abc,(“Thread3”,))

_thread.start_new_thread(abc,(“Thread4”,))

_thread.start_new_thread(abc,(“Thread5”,))

_thread.start_new_thread(abc,(“Thread6”,))

while True:

pass

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Thread Name: Thread1

Thread Name: Thread6

Thread Name: Thread5

Thread Name: Thread2

Thread Name: Thread3

Thread Name: Thread4

■ Creating threads by using _thread module is a low-level threading.

■ In case of creating a child thread by using threading module, the Thread class is used by the following procedure

o Defining the new subclass from the Thread class in threading module.

o Method overriding of __init__() to provide new arguments to the child thread.

o Method overriding of run() to provide the action of the thread.

import threading

class newthread(threading.Thread):

def __init__(self,tname):

threading.Thread.__init__(self)

self.name=tname

t1=newthread(“t1”)

print(“t1 thread is created”)

t2=newthread(“t2”)

print(“t2 thread is created”)

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

t1 thread is created

t2 thread is created

■ The threading module contains a class called as Thread. The Thread class contains various methods as listed below.

o The thread activity is started with the start() method.

import threading

class newthread(threading.Thread):

def __init__(self,tname):

threading.Thread.__init__(self)

self.name=tname

print(“Name in __int__: “,self.name)

t1=newthread(“t1”)

t2=newthread(“t2”)

t1.start()

t2.start()

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Name in __int__: t1

Name in __int__: t2

o The run() method will perform the actual execution of the thread. Whenever a new thread is created, new definition for the run() method can be provided by using method overriding.

import threading

class newthread(threading.Thread):

def __init__(self,tname):

threading.Thread.__init__(self)

self.name=tname

def run(self):

print(self.name,” thread is running”)

t1=newthread(“t1”)

t2=newthread(“t2”)

t1.start()

t2.start()

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

t1 thread is running

t2 thread is running

o The getname() method is used to return the name of the thread.

import threading

class newthread(threading.Thread):

def __init__(self,tname):

threading.Thread.__init__(self)

self.name=tname

def run(self):

print(self.name,” thread is running”)

t1=newthread(“t1”)

t2=newthread(“t2”)

t1.start()

t2.start()

print(“Result of getname(): “,t1.getName())

print(“Result of getname(): “,t2.getName())

t1 thread is running

t2 thread is running

Result of getname(): t2

o The setname() method is used to set a new name for the thread.

import threading

class newthread(threading.Thread):

def __init__(self,tname):

threading.Thread.__init__(self)

self.name=tname

def run(self):

print(self.name,” thread is running”)

t1=newthread(“t1”)

t2=newthread(“t2”)

t1.start()

t2.start()

print(“Result before setname(): “,t1.getName())

print(“Result before setname(): “,t2.getName())

t1.setName(“thread1”)

t2.setName(“thread2”)

print(“Result after setname(): “,t1.getName())

print(“Result after setname(): “,t2.getName())

Output:

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

t1 thread is running

t2 thread is running

Result before setname(): t1

Result before setname(): t2

Result after setname(): thread1

Result after setname(): thread2

o is_alive() method checks whether the thread is alive or terminated. It returns True when the thread is alive; otherwise it returns False.

import threading

class newthread(threading.Thread):

def __init__(self,tname):

threading.Thread.__init__(self)

self.name=tname

def run(self):

print(self.name,” thread is running”)

t1=newthread(“t1”)

t2=newthread(“t2”)

print(“Active threads count: “, threading.active_count())

print(“result of is_alive(t1): “,t1.is_alive())

print(“result of is_alive(t2): “,t2.is_alive())

t1.start()

t2.start()

print(“Active threads count: “, threading.active_count())

print(“result of is_alive(t1): “,t1.is_alive())

print(“result of is_alive(t2): “,t2.is_alive())

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Active threads count: 1

result of is_alive(t1): False

result of is_alive(t2): False

t1 thread is running

t2 thread is running

Active threads count: 2

result of is_alive(t1): False

result of is_alive(t2): False

o Waiting of termination of thread is handled by using join() method.

o isDaemon() method is used to check whether the input thread is a Daemon thread or not.

o setDaemon() method is used to set the thread as Daemon.

■ In Python programming, synchronization is achieved with locks. If one thread locks then other threads should wait until the lock is opened. Thus, locks are an efficient way of supporting thread synchronization. A thread lock can be in any one of the two states as given below.

o Locked

o Unlocked

When the lock is created for a thread, then the default state is “unlocked”. A new lock can be created by using lock() function in threading module.

On a thread lock, the following two functions can be applicable.

■ acquire():If any of the lock is in unlocked state, then any thread can get the lock by using acquire() method.

■ release():If any of the lock is in “locked state” then the corresponding thread can release the lock by using release() method.

import threading

class newthread(threading.Thread):

def __init__(self,tname,l):

threading.Thread.__init__(self)

self.name=tname

self.lock=l

def run(self):

print(self.name,” thread is running”)

def x(self):

self.lock.acquire()

print(self.name, “is locked”)

def y(self):

self.lock.release()

print(self.name, “is released”)

l=threading.Lock()

t1=newthread(“t1”,l)

t2=newthread(«t2»,l)

t1.start()

t2.start()

t1.x()

t1.y()

t2.x()

t2.y()

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

t1 thread is running

t2 thread is running

t1 is locked

t1 is released

t2 is locked

t2 is released

The Lock() method allows the thread to acquire only one lock. The lock can be acquired by another thread only after it has been released by the current thread.

import threading

class newthread(threading.Thread):

def __init__(self,tname,l):

threading.Thread.__init__(self)

self.name=tname

self.lock=l

def run(self):

print(self.name,” thread is running”)

def x(self):

self.lock.acquire()

print(self.name, “is locked”)

def y(self):

self.lock.release()

print(self.name, “is released”)

l=threading.Lock()

t1=newthread(“t1”,l)

t2=newthread(«t2»,l)

t1.start()

t2.start()

t1.x()

t2.x()

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

t1 thread is running

t2 thread is running

t1 is locked

To overcome this problem, locks are created using the RLock()method. These locks are called as reentrant locks.

import threading

class newthread(threading.Thread):

def __init__(self,tname,l):

threading.Thread.__init__(self)

self.name=tname

self.lock=l

def run(self):

print(self.name,” thread is running”)

def x(self):

self.lock.acquire()

print(self.name, “is locked”)

def y(self):

self.lock.release()

print(self.name, “is released”)

l=threading.RLock()

t1=newthread(“t1”,l)

t2=newthread(«t2»,l)

t1.start()

t2.start()

t1.x()

t2.x()

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

t1 thread is running

t2 thread is running

t1 is locked

t2 is locked

Program 1: Write a Python program to exit the thread by using _thread module.

import _thread

def abc(arg1):

print(“Started Thread Name: “,arg1)

print(“The thread1 is now exit”)

_thread.exit()

_thread.start_new_thread(abc,(“Thread1”,))

while True:

pass

Output

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

Started Thread Name: Thread1

The thread1 is now exit

Glossary

■ A thread is a light-weight process. Multiple threads can share the memory space of the associated processes.

■ A process is called as heavy-weight task while a thread is called as light-weight task. Here, weight represents the memory usage.

Summary

■ In Python, threads can be created by using _thread or threading modules.

■ The _thread uses low-level threading and threading module uses high-level threading.

■ Various operations can be applied on the thread. The threading module consists of Thread class and it contains various methods.

■ Thread synchronization is achieved with locks. A lock can be created. The created object can acquire an additional lock or release a lock.

CHAPTER

  

Tkinter

16

  

Python supports Graphical User Interface (GUI) by using wxPython, JPython and Tkinter. Among these, the present chapter discuses about Tkinter based GUI. The tkinter module contains various classes for supporting widgets.

16.1 Introduction

The earlier chapters of this book discussed command line interface parameters in Python. However, Python programming supports both the command line interface and graphical user interface (GUI). The Python supports GUI in three ways viz., wxPython, JPython and Tkinter.

■ The wxPython is a platform independent toolkit for supporting GUI in Python. The implementation of GUI with this toolkit is easy, but since wxPython gets frequently updated, frequent patch updations are required for new feature requests.

■ The JPython supports GUI for integrating with the Java platform. It is majorly used for embedded scripting, interactive interpreter and rapid application development.

■ The Tkinter is also called as Tk interface. It is a standard toolkit in Python for GUI. It binds the Tk widgets to Python objects. To use this, a working Tcl/Tk system is required. The advantage of Tkinter is that it is more portable than other GUI toolkits.

In Python 3, the tkinter module is responsible for handling the various operations of GUI. It provides an easy way to create GUI applications in Python. In case of using this toolkit, from tkinter import * statement should be included. With this, all the attributes of tkinter module are available in the current program. After importing, create a GUI application main window. For this, use Tk() method. Then add the required widgets to the main window. Finally, call the mainloop() method for executing the events.

from tkinter import *

a = Tk()

a.title(‘First Tkinter program’)

a.mainloop()

Output:

D:\PythonPrograms>python a1.py

16.2 Widget

Tkinter widgets represent various objects for handling GUI. For each GUI component, an object is maintained for the corresponding widget class. Various widgets are listed below

■ button

■ canvas

■ checckbutton

■ combobox

■ entry

■ frame

■ label

■ labelframe

■ listbox

■ menu

■ menubutton

■ message

■ notebook

■ optionMenu

■ panedwindow

■ progressbar

■ radiobuttion

■ scale

■ scrollbar

■ separator

■ sizegrip

■ spinbox

■ text

■ treeview

The common properties of widgets include colors, fonts, dimensions, anchors, bitmaps and cursors. The tkinker places all the components in the main window by using any one of the following methods:

■ pack() method places components in blocks of parent widget.

■ grid() method places components in table structure of parent widget.

■ place() method places components within the specified position of parent widget.

16.3 Label Widget

The label widget is used to add the labels and images in the GUI window. For this widget component, Label class is used.

from tkinter import *

a = Tk()

l = Label(a, text=”This is a Label”)

l.pack()

a.title(‘First Tkinter program’)

a.mainloop()

Output

In addition to adding text, the label widget is also used to add images in the GUI window. For this, the image should be represented with PhotoImage() method.

from tkinter import *

a = Tk()

i=PhotoImage(file=”D:\index.gif”)

l2=Label(a,image=i).pack()

a.title(‘First Tkinter program’)

a.mainloop()

Output

16.4 Button Widget

The button widget is used to add a button in the GUI window.

from tkinter import *

def abc():

print(“OK button is clicked”)

a = Tk()

b1=Button(a, text=”OK”, width=30, command=abc).pack()

a.mainloop()

Output

In case of buttons, the arguments can be passed to the command function by using lambda expressions.

from tkinter import *

def abc(number):

print(number, “button is clicked”)

a = Tk()

b1=Button(a, text=”OK”, width=30, command=lambda:abc(1)).pack()

b2=Button(a, text=”RESET”, width=30, command=lambda:abc(2)).pack()

a.mainloop()

Output

In the above example code, the command for the button widget is a function. Other than function, method can also be used for representing the command for the widget. These types of methods are called as “Bound Methods”. They should be defined within a class. In case of using bound methods, __init__ method should be overridden.

from tkinter import *

class xyz:

def abc(number):

print(number, “button is clicked”)

a = Tk()

b1=Button(a, text=”OK”, width=30, command=lambda:xyz.abc(1)).pack()

b2=Button(a, text=”RESET”, width=30, command=lambda:xyz.abc(2)).pack()

a.mainloop()

Output:

16.5 Checkbutton Widget

The Checkbutton widget is used to add the checkbutton in the GUI window. It allows multiple selections of options.

from tkinter import *

a = Tk()

var1=IntVar()

var2=IntVar()

var3=IntVar()

var4=IntVar()

c1=Checkbutton(a, text=”Value1”, variable=var1).pack()

c2=Checkbutton(a, text=”Value2”, variable=var2).pack()

c3=Checkbutton(a, text=”Value3”, variable=var3).pack()

c4=Checkbutton(a, text=”Value4”, variable=var4).pack()

print(“Value of var1: “,var1)

print(“Value of var2: “,var2)

print(“Value of var3: “,var3)

print(“Value of var4: “,var4)

a.mainloop()

Output

16.6 Entry Widget

The entry widget is used to add the textbox in the GUI window. The entry widget will take single line text input from the user.

Output

from tkinter import *

a = Tk()

l1=Label(a, text=”Enter your name”).pack()

e1=Entry(a).pack()

b1=Button(a,text=”SUBMIT”).pack()

16.7 Listbox Widget

The Listbox widget is used to add the drop down list box component in the GUI window. It allows user to select multiple options. The elements can be added with insert() method and deleted with delete() method.

from tkinter import *

a = Tk()

l1=Listbox(a,height=5)

l1.pack()

l1.insert(END, “item1”)

l1.insert(END, “item2”)

l1.insert(END, “item3”)

l1.insert(END, “item4”)

l1.insert(END, “item5”)

a.mainloop()

Output

16.8 Radiobutton Widget

The Radiobutton widget is used to add combo of options (multiple options) in the GUI window. It allows only option selection among multiple options.

from tkinter import *

a = Tk()

var = IntVar()

Radiobutton(a, text=”radio1”, variable=var, value=1).pack(anchor=W)

Radiobutton(a, text=”radio2”, variable=var, value=2).pack(anchor=W)

Radiobutton(a, text=”radio3”, variable=var, value=3).pack(anchor=W)

a.mainloop()

Output

16.9 Scrollbar Widget

The Scrollbar widget is used to add the scrollbar component in the GUI window.

from tkinter import *

scrollbar = Scrollbar(a)

scrollbar.pack(side=RIGHT, fill=Y)

a.mainloop()

16.10 Text Widget

The Text widget is used to add multiple lines of text in the GUI window.

from tkinter import *

a = Tk()

t1 = Text(a, height=3, width=50)

t1.pack()

t1.insert(END, “This is first line\n This is second line\n This is third line”)

a.mainloop()

Output

16.11 Container Widgets

Container widgets are used to place the widget objects in a proper order. In general, they are also called as layout managers. For this, it can use grid widget or frame widget. The grid layout widget is found to be more efficient than pack(). The grid consists of various rows and columns. If any widget is placed with the grid widget, then the position of the cell in the grid should be specified with the corresponding row and column numbers.

from tkinter import *

a = Tk()

l1=Label(a, text=”Enter user name”).grid(row=0,column=0)

e1=Entry(a)

e1.grid(row=0,column=1)

l2=Label(a, text=”Enter password”).grid(row=1,column=0)

e2=Entry(a)

e2.grid(row=1,column=1)

b1=Button(a,text=”SUBMIT”).grid(row=2,column=0)

a.mainloop()

Output

16.12 Frame Widget

The Frame widget is used to arrange other widgets in the container. In general, the other widgets are under the control of geometry manager.

from tkinter import *

a = Tk()

def abc():

#print(e1.get())

te=e1.get()

f=Frame(a)

f.pack()

l=Label(f, text=te).pack()

l1=Label(a, text=”Enter your name”).pack()

e1=Entry(a)

e1.pack()

b1=Button(a,text=”SUBMIT”, command=abc).pack()

a.mainloop()

Output

16.13 Menu Widget

The menu widget is used to create a new menu or various menu items. Each menu item can also have sub-menu items.

from tkinter import *

a = Tk()

def open1():

print(“File Menu is selcted”)

def print():

print(“File Menu is selcted”)

me=Menu(a)

mainmenu = Menu(me)

filemenu=Menu(me)

filemenu.add_command(label=»Open», command=open1)

filemenu.add_command(label=»print», command=print)

mainmenu.add_cascade(label=»File», menu=filemenu)

mainmenu.add_command(label=”Exit”, command=a.quit)

a.config(menu=mainmenu)

a.mainloop()

Output

16.14 Labelframe Widget

The LabelFrame widget is a variant of Frame widget. The LabelFrame widget draws a border around its child widgets. It also contains the title display property. The LabelFrame widget is used for grouping multiple related widgets.

from tkinter import *

m = Tk()

g = LabelFrame(m, text=”Entry widget group”, padx=10, pady=10)

g.pack(padx=20, pady=20)

w1 = Entry(g)

w1.pack()

w2 = Entry(g)

w2.pack()

mainloop()

Output

16.15 Message Widget

The message widget is used to display multiple lines of text. It is a variant of Label widget where the Label widget is used to display single widget.

from tkinter import *

m = Tk()

w = Message(m, text=”this is a message widget-multi line message”)

w.pack()

mainloop()

Output

16.16 Combobox Widget

The Combobox widget is used to add the drop-down selection element in the window.

from tkinter import *
